**CSV import characterization correction**: closer inspection during implementation found that `CreateLeadFromImport::handle()` (the public entry point, one level above the `processRow()` method this fix touches) already wraps row processing in its own `try { ... } catch (Throwable $e) { recordRow(..., Failed, ...) }` — a `DeadlockException` from this path was therefore already caught and recorded as a `Failed` import row, not left to crash the whole chunk job as this finding's own Architecture Brief had initially assumed. This is a correction in the CSV import path's favor, discovered, not introduced — **no code change resulted from this discovery**; the existing per-row catch was already present and remains completely untouched.

**Explicitly deferred, remaining open — NOT addressed by this closure**: the email ingestion path's `\Seen`-before-permanent-loss recovery gap (`ProcessEmailLeadSource::handle()` flags a message `\Seen` even when its processing fails, so a deadlock — now far rarer, but not the only possible failure cause — caught there still permanently loses that one inbound lead email with no automatic recovery); and CSV import's chunk-retry duplicate-safety (whether a retried `ReadChunk` job re-processing an already-partially-completed chunk could create duplicate rows) — not independently verified end-to-end in this closure, though the correction above somewhat de-risks it. Both remain genuinely open, unresolved, and are candidates for their own separate future investigations.

**Exact files changed**: `app/Http/Controllers/Api/LeadWebhookController.php` (`createLead()` only), `app/Actions/Tenant/CreateManualLead.php` (`handle()` only), `app/Actions/Tenant/CreateLeadFromImport.php` (`handle()`'s transaction closure only), `app/Jobs/ProcessEmailLeadSource.php` (`createLeadFromEmail()` only). Four files total — each change is exactly the `sharedLock()` → `lockForUpdate()` substitution plus an explanatory comment; nothing else was moved, reordered, or restructured. No test file was modified (the existing `StatusPipelineLockCoordinationTest.php` already provides deterministic structural regression coverage; the real deadlock-elimination proof required real PostgreSQL concurrency, which cannot be encoded in the SQLite-based CI suite, so it was performed manually and is documented here). No migration.

**Explicitly excluded from this closure's scope**: `Lead::nextIdentifier()` — intentionally unchanged; `toggleLeadStatus()`'s writer-side locking, `LeadAssignmentService`, `LeadMergeService` — all confirmed untouched; the email `\Seen` recovery gap and CSV chunk-retry duplicate-safety question (above) — explicitly deferred, not fixed, not marked resolved; SEC-1, REL-1, and REL-2 — all confirmed untouched by this closure.

**This finding is CLOSED.** Do not reopen unless a future change causes a real regression. Every other finding surfaced by the fresh forensic audit (DB-1, DB-2, UX-1, PROD-1, SEC-2, QA-1, QA-2, ARCH-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, REL-3, REL-4, OPS-1, OPS-2), plus the email `\Seen` recovery gap and CSV chunk-retry duplicate-safety question named above, remain open and are explicitly **not** addressed by this closure.

### EMAIL-LOSS-1 — Email `\Seen`-Before-Durable-Failure Recovery Gap — **COMPLETE / APPROVED / CLOSED**

Discovered during DEADLOCK-1's own inspection (named there as a distinct, explicitly-deferred finding, not part of that closure), investigated and closed as its own separate forensic item via the full Inspect → Impact Analysis → Architecture/Decision Brief → Approval → Implementation → Testing → Regression → Final Verification → Approval sequence — including a mid-flight, user-caught correction cycle of its own (see "Event Replay correction" below). **Severity: reliability/data-loss. Confidence: HIGH.**

**Original finding**: `ProcessEmailLeadSource::handle()` flagged an inbound email message `\Seen` **unconditionally**, whether processing of that message succeeded or failed — a per-message `try`/`catch` logged the failure but the very next statement marked the message `\Seen` regardless. Since IMAP's own `UNSEEN` search is the *only* mechanism the normal poll uses to find work, a message marked `\Seen` after a failed attempt could never be re-discovered by any future scheduled run — a transient failure (a DB deadlock, a momentary connection blip) became **permanent, silent lead loss**, with only a log line as evidence anything happened. Genuinely reproduced via direct code trace and elementary PHP semantics verification (a live IMAP reproduction was determined, and stated explicitly, not to be feasible — `IMAP\Connection` is a `final` internal class constructible only by a real `imap_open()`, and no local disposable IMAP test server was available; no real/external mailbox was touched).

**Remediation — durable recovery mechanism**:
- A new `FailedEmailMessage` model/table records a failure **before** `\Seen` is applied — never the reverse. If the durable write itself fails, the message is deliberately left unseen (a rare, doubly-unlucky case, but strictly safer than losing the only trace that anything happened) rather than ever risking `\Seen` with no corresponding recovery record.
- **Stable identity**: both IMAP UID and its governing UIDVALIDITY are captured together as the primary, safe replay key (`unique(['lead_source_id', 'uidvalidity', 'imap_uid'])`) — a bare UID is not globally or even mailbox-stable across sessions per RFC 3501, so UID alone was never used. The RFC 5322 Message-ID header is captured as a secondary, defense-in-depth cross-check (deliberately excluded from the unique constraint, since it can be missing/malformed on some senders' mail) — sender email address alone was never used as a replay identity.
- **Recovery interface**: a new `leads:retry-failed-emails {--limit=25}` Artisan command (deliberately no admin UI in this phase, per the approved scope) selects due `Failed` records, groups by source, and dispatches `ProcessEmailLeadSource` in a retry mode that fully reuses the job's own existing `WithoutOverlapping`/`$tries`/IMAP-connection-lifecycle machinery — no new locking was invented. Scheduled at `everyFifteenMinutes()` (`routes/console.php`), deliberately far less frequent than the per-minute normal poll, since the ceiling/backoff below already spaces out individual retries.
- **Retry ceiling and backoff**: a uniform `MAX_RETRY_ATTEMPTS = 5`, `RETRY_BACKOFF_MINUTES = 15` × attempts already made — preserving the pre-existing, deliberate poison-message protection (a permanently-malformed message is retried a small, bounded number of times, then durably marked `Exhausted` — never deleted, never silently dropped, never infinitely retried).
- At retry time, three independent safety gates must all pass before any replay is attempted: the mailbox's *current* UIDVALIDITY must still match the one recorded at failure time; the message's live sequence number must still resolve from its stored UID (`imap_msgno()`); and, when both sides have one, the re-read Message-ID must still match what was originally recorded. Any mismatch marks the record `Exhausted` rather than risking a replay against a possibly-different message.

**Event Replay correction — a defect found and fixed within this same closure, not carried forward**: the first implementation pass conflated two genuinely different post-persist failure causes — the `LeadReceived`/`LeadUpdated` event dispatch failing, versus attachment saving failing — under one `partial_lead_id` marker. A dedicated Final Verification pass (performed before this closure, per the standing workflow) correctly caught that this meant an event-dispatch failure could reach a false `Recovered` state via an attachment-only retry, permanently and silently skipping auto-assignment, scoring, and the new-lead notification for an otherwise-correctly-persisted Lead — confirmed, via direct listener inspection, to be a real risk: `AutoAssignLead`/`LeadAssignmentService::autoAssign()` and `SendNewLeadNotification` were independently verified to have **no** existing idempotency guard against a duplicate `LeadReceived`, so the fix could not simply re-fire the event unconditionally on any retry either. The approved fix (Option 1 of that follow-up Decision Brief) split the single try/catch into two independent ones, each tagging `EmailMessagePartiallyProcessed` with a new `EmailPartialFailureStage` (`Event`/`Attachments`) and whether the original operation was a create or an append — both persisted durably on `FailedEmailMessage` (`partial_stage`, `is_new_lead`). A retry now re-fires the event **only** when the persisted stage confirms it never dispatched, always firing the *correct* class (`LeadReceived` for an original create, `LeadUpdated` for an original append) — and **never** re-fires an event whose stage confirms it already dispatched successfully. `AutoAssignLead`, `LeadAssignmentService`, `RecomputeLeadScoreOnReceipt`, `SendNewLeadNotification`, and every other `LeadReceived`/`LeadUpdated` listener were confirmed completely untouched — the fix guarantees the event fires exactly once, rather than making any listener itself safe to call twice.

**Verification**:
- Direct, adversarial code trace of the full failure matrix (Lead-creation failure before persistence; create-then-event-success-then-attachment-failure; create-then-event-failure; the append-path equivalents of both; and retries of each) confirmed correct in every branch, including the event correctly advancing from `Event` to `Attachments` stage if it succeeds on retry but attachments then fail again, and an event-stage failure still correctly reaching `Exhausted` at the retry ceiling with the underlying Lead confirmed unaffected.
- A dedicated falsification pass explicitly tried to find a branch where an event could misclassify, fire twice, mark `Recovered` despite failing, or where create/append could re-run during a partial replay, or where stage/action state could be lost on a retry-failure update — none found. One narrow, pre-existing, non-blocking discrepancy was noted (a `->fresh()` status re-check whose result isn't reassigned before use) and confirmed provably inert under this job's actual, exclusively-used `WithoutOverlapping` + single-threaded-execution guarantees — not introduced or worsened by this closure, not treated as blocking.
- `tests/Feature/Leads/FailedEmailMessageRetryTest.php` (22 tests) plus 6 new Reflection-based tests in `tests/Unit/ProcessEmailLeadSourceBodyExtractionTest.php`: all passing, stable across repeated runs — covering the durable-record model/migration/factory, the retry command's selection/grouping/bounding logic (via `Bus::fake()`), the retry ceiling/backoff/exhaustion state machine, and — specifically for the Event Replay correction — the exact `eventAlreadyDispatchedFor()` decision (Attachments-stage → true; Event-stage or null → false) and `recordRetryFailure()`'s stage-advancing/stage-preserving behavior.
- Existing `FetchEmailLeadsTest.php` and the full `ProcessEmailLeadSourceBodyExtractionTest.php` suite: unchanged in behavior, all still passing.
- DEADLOCK-1/REL-1/REL-2/SEC-1's own regression suites (`StatusPipelineLockCoordinationTest.php`, `WebhookTest.php`, `WebsiteFormSubmissionTest.php`, `ManualLeadCreationTest.php`, `LeadCsvImportEngineTest.php`, `FacebookLeadAdsSpreadsheetTest.php`): all passing, confirming zero regression to any prior closure.
- Full `tests/Feature/Leads/` + `tests/Feature/Broadcasting/` (broader regression, re-run repeatedly fresh): unchanged from the established baseline — the same 2 pre-existing, unrelated `LeadsIndexListenerTest` failures, the same 1 known pre-existing IMAP-extension-unavailable-required skip. No new failure at any point across this finding's full multi-pass history.
- Pint: clean, every run. PHPStan across every new/touched production file: zero net-new diagnostics — the one diagnostic present throughout (a pre-existing, unrelated `missingType.iterableValue` on `appendEmailMessage()`'s `$body` parameter, carried unchanged since before REL-1) confirmed identical via repeated `git stash` baseline comparison, only ever shifting line number as code was added above it.
- `git diff --check`: clean throughout.

**No claim stronger than the verification evidence supports**: no real, end-to-end IMAP integration test was ever performed or claimed — `IMAP\Connection`'s `final`, internal, constructor-only-via-`imap_open()` nature made this infeasible in this environment, and no real/external mailbox was used to avoid irreversible side effects on real mail state. Every test added is a deterministic, DB/state-level test of the logic that does not require a live `Connection`; the full `handle()`/`createLeadFromEmail()`/`handleRetries()`/`runPostPersistSteps()` call chains are verified by direct, repeated code trace, not by execution.

**Exact files changed**: `app/Jobs/ProcessEmailLeadSource.php` (`handle()` restructured into `handleUnseenMessages()`/`handleRetries()`/`retryOneMessage()`; new `tryRecordNewFailure()`, `recordRetryFailure()`, `exhaustRecord()`, `mailboxUidValidity()`, `mailboxConnectionString()`, `runPostPersistSteps()`, `eventAlreadyDispatchedFor()`, `classifyFailure()`, `normalizeMessageId()`; `createLeadFromEmail()`/`appendEmailMessage()` preserved in shape, delegating their post-persist step to the new shared method). New: `app/Models/FailedEmailMessage.php`, `app/Enums/FailedEmailMessageStatus.php`, `app/Enums/EmailPartialFailureStage.php`, `app/Exceptions/EmailMessagePartiallyProcessed.php`, `database/migrations/2026_09_22_060000_create_failed_email_messages_table.php`, `database/factories/FailedEmailMessageFactory.php`, `app/Console/Commands/RetryFailedEmailMessages.php`, `tests/Feature/Leads/FailedEmailMessageRetryTest.php`. Modified: `routes/console.php` (one new scheduled line), `tests/Unit/ProcessEmailLeadSourceBodyExtractionTest.php` (6 new tests appended).

**Explicitly deferred, remaining open — NOT addressed by this closure**: the pre-existing multi-attachment mid-loop duplicate-`LeadAttachment` risk in `saveAttachments()` (a retry of the attachments step alone can re-save an already-successfully-saved earlier attachment if a later one in the same message fails) — noted during this finding's own investigation, not fixed, not introduced by it. The retry record-write-failure edge case (`recordRetryFailure()`/`exhaustRecord()`'s own DB write is not itself wrapped in a try/catch during retry) — non-data-loss (the record already existed and remains in its prior, correct, retryable state), not fixed. The IMAP UIDVALIDITY-mailbox-reconfiguration edge case (a coincidental UIDVALIDITY collision if a source's credentials are repointed at a different mailbox) — inherent to the protocol, not a defect in this implementation, not addressed. CSV import's chunk-retry duplicate-safety question (named during DEADLOCK-1, restated as still open above) — untouched by this closure, remains its own separate, unresolved question. The Messenger/Instagram/WhatsApp/Facebook-Lead-Ads "always HTTP 200 regardless of outcome" same-pattern risk (noted as structurally analogous during this finding's own inspection) — not investigated further, not fixed, a candidate for its own separate future finding.

**No blanket claim that email ingestion is now fully fault-tolerant**: this closure guarantees a transient failure is durably recoverable and that recovery does not duplicate or omit a Lead's core persisted data or its exactly-once event dispatch — it does not claim to have eliminated every conceivable failure mode in this file (see the residual risks above).

**This finding is CLOSED.** Do not reopen unless a future change causes a real regression. Every other finding surfaced by the fresh forensic audit (DB-1, DB-2, UX-1, PROD-1, SEC-2, QA-1, QA-2, ARCH-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, REL-3, REL-4, OPS-1, OPS-2), plus CSV import's chunk-retry duplicate-safety question and the Messenger/Instagram/WhatsApp/Facebook-Lead-Ads always-200 same-pattern risk named above, remain open and are explicitly **not** addressed by this closure.

### DB-1 — Lead Hard Deletion Had No Durable Deletion Audit — **COMPLETE / APPROVED / CLOSED**

One of the findings surfaced by the same fresh forensic audit that produced SEC-1/REL-1/REL-2/DEADLOCK-1/EMAIL-LOSS-1 above, investigated and closed via the full Inspect → Impact Analysis → Architecture/Decision Brief → Approval → Implementation → Testing → Regression → Final Verification → Approval sequence — including a full second cycle of that same sequence for a concurrency blocker discovered during this finding's own first Final Verification pass (see "Concurrency blocker" below), before final closure. **Severity: data-integrity / auditability. Confidence: HIGH.**

**Finding**: `Lead` deletion (`Index::delete()`, `Index::performBulkDelete()`, `Show::delete()`) was, and remains, a permanent hard delete with no `SoftDeletes` and no restore workflow — a deliberate, unchanged product decision. But before this closure, a hard delete left **no durable record that it happened at all**: no who, no when, no what was deleted, and — because `lead_consents`/`lead_consent_events` cascade-deleted along with the Lead — a tenant's entire consent grant/withdrawal history for that Lead vanished with it, with zero trace.

**Approved product decisions (unchanged, confirmed still in force)**: hard delete remains permanent — no `SoftDeletes`, no restore workflow, was never proposed and was not introduced. Historical deletions (everything deleted before this closure) are explicitly out of scope — no reconstruction of previously deleted Leads was attempted or is possible. `lead_duplicate_links` loss on Lead deletion remains an accepted, unrecoverable side effect — no tombstone, cascade behavior unchanged. Bulk-delete partial-failure exception handling (`performBulkDelete()`'s existing per-lead try-less loop and its `$succeeded` counter) is explicitly a separate, out-of-scope reliability question — not redesigned, not touched beyond the minimum required by this closure (see below).

**Remediation — durable audit**: a new `LeadDeletionAudit` model/table (ULID PK, `tenant_id`, nullable `lead_id`/`nullOnDelete`, `lead_identifier`/`lead_name` non-null snapshots, nullable `lead_email`/`lead_phone`, `source_type`, nullable `deleted_by`/`nullOnDelete`, `is_bulk`, nullable `cascade_summary` JSON, indexed on `tenant_id`/`lead_id`/`deleted_by`) is the single permanent trace of a deleted Lead's identity. `App\Actions\Tenant\RecordLeadDeletionAudit` is the single shared primitive all three delete call sites go through, inside the SAME `DB::transaction()` as the Lead's own delete — never a separate one: audit row created, then `lead_consents`/`lead_consent_events` stamped with the identity snapshot, then `$lead->delete()`, all before `COMMIT`. Deliberately no try/catch anywhere in this chain — if the audit write or consent snapshot throws for any reason, that exception propagates straight out to the caller's transaction and rolls it back, so the Lead is never deleted without a durable audit row (fail-closed); no custom failure-swallowing catch was introduced. `cascade_summary` is a per-table count (attachments, notes, assignments, status logs, detail-update logs, score logs, tasks, deals, tags, custom field values, conversations, messages, duplicate links), computed from real relation counts before the delete, covering only genuinely cascade-DESTROYED tables — never message body/HTML/MIME/attachment binary/credential content.

**Remediation — consent preservation**: `lead_consents` and `lead_consent_events` no longer cascade-delete with their Lead. Both tables' `lead_id` FK moved from `cascadeOnDelete()` to nullable + `nullOnDelete()` (migration ordering: drop the existing FK constraint, relax nullability, add the new FK — verified safe on both PostgreSQL and the SQLite test driver), and both gained nullable `lead_identifier`/`lead_name` snapshot columns. Those two columns are populated **only** by `RecordLeadDeletionAudit` at the moment of deletion — `App\Actions\Tenant\RecordLeadConsent` (ordinary grant/withdraw) was left completely untouched and never touches them. After a Lead is deleted, its consent rows survive with `lead_id = NULL` and the snapshot fields populated, remain directly queryable by `lead_identifier`, and multiple detached rows sharing the same `consent_type` across different deleted Leads coexist without violating `lead_consents`' pre-existing `unique(lead_id, consent_type)` constraint — standard SQL NULL-distinctness, confirmed empirically against the real PostgreSQL connection, not merely assumed. Ordinary live-Lead consent grant/withdraw, the consent card, consent history, and `LeadMergeService`'s consent-merging logic are all confirmed unaffected — `LeadMergeService.php` was never modified.

**Concurrency blocker (discovered during this finding's own first Final Verification pass, closed via its own follow-up Architecture Brief → Approval → Implementation → Testing → Final Verification → Approval cycle before this overall closure)**: none of the three delete call sites acquired a row lock on the Lead before writing its audit, so two concurrent delete requests for the same Lead (a double-click, two tabs, a client retry) could both pass authorization on independently-loaded Lead objects and both durably write a `LeadDeletionAudit` row, even though only one of them could physically delete the row — one true deletion, two misleading audit rows. Fixed by moving the concurrency-safety logic into the single shared primitive: `RecordLeadDeletionAudit::handle()` now begins by re-fetching the Lead **inside** the caller's transaction via `Lead::where('tenant_id', ...)->whereKey($lead->id)->lockForUpdate()->first()` — never trusting the possibly-stale, pre-transaction Lead instance passed in — and returns `null` (no audit, no consent snapshot, no side effect at all) if that locked re-fetch finds the row already gone. All three call sites gained exactly one `if (handle() === null) { return; }` guard before their existing `$lead->delete()` call; a race-losing request is treated as successfully satisfied (the Lead is already gone) with no new user-facing error state invented. This is the same `Model::whereKey($id)->lockForUpdate()->first()/firstOrFail()` idiom already established throughout this codebase (`Lead::nextIdentifier()`, `CreateManualLead`, `LeadSources::toggle*()`, `LeadMergeService::merge()`'s own M8 guard) — no new, generic locking infrastructure was introduced. The fix acquires exactly one lock per transaction and never a second one, so it cannot participate in a circular wait with `LeadMergeService`'s two-row lock, any `Tenant`-row lock, or any other existing lock in this codebase — confirmed by direct inspection of every `lockForUpdate()` call site; **DEADLOCK-1 was not touched, not reopened.**

**Concurrency verification — real PostgreSQL, not just SQLite structural checks**: `tests/Feature/Leads/LeadDeletionAuditConcurrencyTest.php` opens two genuinely independent named database connections to the real dev database and proves actual cross-session exclusivity via `FOR UPDATE` / `FOR UPDATE NOWAIT` — session A holds the lock, session B's immediate NOWAIT attempt is deterministically rejected, and succeeds the instant A commits; deterministic and instant (no sleep/poll/timing race), skips cleanly (never a false pass) when no real, reachable PostgreSQL connection is available, and cannot be satisfied by SQLite (whose grammar silently no-ops every lock clause). Beyond that automated proof, a disposable, non-committed `pcntl_fork()` verification script (not part of the repository) ran **8 rounds** of two genuinely separate OS processes racing to delete the identical Lead against the real dev database: **8/8 rounds** produced exactly one `LeadDeletionAudit` row, the Lead physically gone exactly once, both `lead_consents`/`lead_consent_events` rows detached exactly once, zero exceptions, and zero deadlocks — with the winning process alternating between parent and child across rounds, confirming a genuine race rather than a fixed-ordering artifact. The real dev database was independently confirmed, via read-only queries, to be left at zero residual rows after every verification pass.

**Verification**:
- `LeadDeletionAuditTest.php`: **16/16 passed** (10 covering audit-field correctness, snapshot accuracy, bulk `is_bulk` tagging, fail-closed audit/consent-failure rollback, detached-consent identifiability, cascade-count accuracy, and unchanged duplicate-link/notes/deals cascades; 6 covering the concurrency fix's nullable-return contract — existing-Lead success, missing-Lead no-op, missing-Lead skips the consent snapshot, and all three call sites correctly skip `$lead->delete()` when the audit action reports the Lead already gone).
- `LeadDeletionAuditConcurrencyTest.php`: **1/1 passed** against the real PostgreSQL connection (not skipped), stable across repeated runs.
- Combined focused suite (`LeadDeletionAuditTest`, `LeadDeletionTest`, `BulkActionsTest`, `LeadConsentTest`, `LeadConsentManagementTest`, `WebsiteFormConsentSubmissionTest`, `EnumConstraintVerifierTest`): **109/109 passed** (110/110 including the real-Postgres concurrency test).
- Full `tests/Feature/Leads/`: **1074 tests, 1073 passed**, 1 known pre-existing environment-conditional skip (unchanged).
- `tests/Feature/Broadcasting/`: same **2 known pre-existing, unrelated `LeadsIndexListenerTest` failures** — confirmed, via `git stash`, to reproduce identically on unmodified `main`; not touched.
- Pint: clean. `git diff --check`: clean throughout every pass.
- PHPStan: repo-wide baseline is **424 pre-existing errors on unmodified `main`** (confirmed via `git stash`, entirely unrelated to this finding). This closure's full diff adds exactly **+3** — all the identical, pre-existing shape (`Auth::user()` resolving to `PlatformUser|TenantUser|null` instead of the guard-specific `TenantUser|null`, a Larastan limitation already present at dozens of sibling call sites in the same two files) — not a genuine type-unsafe path, since these Livewire components are only ever mounted on tenant-side routes behind the `web` guard. No type was widened or cast to silence anything. The concurrency-blocker follow-up added **0 further net-new diagnostics** (still 427 total); `RecordLeadDeletionAudit.php` alone: 0 errors.

**Accidental production-database incident during this finding's work, fully disclosed and resolved before continuing**: an early verification step accidentally ran `migrate:fresh` against the local development PostgreSQL database (`optifin_erp`) instead of an isolated test database, wiping its data. This was immediately disclosed; the user confirmed the local dev database was disposable and approved a rebuild (schema migration + `PermissionSeeder`/`ModuleSeeder` + a fresh Mother Admin), verified successful before implementation resumed. No production or remote database was touched at any point. Not a code defect — recorded here only for a complete, honest record of this finding's history.

**No historical deletion reconstruction was performed or is possible**: this closure is forward-looking only — every Lead deleted before this closure shipped has no audit row and cannot be retroactively reconstructed. **No claim of legal, regulatory, or data-retention compliance is made**: this closure implements exactly the approved product behavior (a durable internal audit trail and consent preservation) and nothing beyond it — it is not represented as satisfying any external compliance obligation the repository does not itself establish.

**Exact files changed**: New — `database/migrations/2026_09_23_060000_create_lead_deletion_audits_table.php`, `database/migrations/2026_09_23_060001_add_deletion_snapshot_columns_to_lead_consents_table.php`, `database/migrations/2026_09_23_060002_add_deletion_snapshot_columns_to_lead_consent_events_table.php`, `app/Models/LeadDeletionAudit.php`, `database/factories/LeadDeletionAuditFactory.php`, `app/Actions/Tenant/RecordLeadDeletionAudit.php`, `tests/Feature/Leads/LeadDeletionAuditTest.php`, `tests/Feature/Leads/LeadDeletionAuditConcurrencyTest.php`. Modified — `app/Livewire/Tenant/Leads/Index.php` (`delete()`, `performBulkDelete()` only), `app/Livewire/Tenant/Leads/Show.php` (`delete()` only), `app/Models/LeadConsent.php`/`app/Models/LeadConsentEvent.php` (`#[Fillable]` + docblocks only), `tests/Feature/Leads/LeadConsentTest.php` (one pre-existing test's cascade-delete expectation updated to the now-approved detach-not-destroy behavior — the only existing assertion changed anywhere in this closure).

**Explicitly excluded from this closure's scope, or left as residual/out-of-scope risk**: `LeadObserver.php`, `LeadPolicy.php`, and `LeadMergeService.php` all confirmed completely untouched. Authorization (`lead-management.delete` permission, Client-Admin-only, tenant isolation), password confirmation, and the individually-deletable-source-type restriction are all unchanged — no new route or permission was introduced. `performBulkDelete()`'s `$succeeded` counter still does not distinguish "this call physically deleted the Lead" from "it was already gone when this call's lock was acquired" under a race — a pre-existing shape, explicitly not redesigned here, consistent with bulk partial-failure handling remaining its own separate, unaddressed reliability question. `LeadMergeService`'s already-merged guard has a narrow, pre-existing, unrelated blind spot if a loser Lead is concurrently deleted (a nullsafe check that doesn't distinguish "not merged" from "row no longer exists") — not introduced or worsened by this closure, not fixed, a candidate for its own separate future investigation. The automated real-PostgreSQL concurrency test proves session-level lock exclusivity; the full end-to-end "exactly one audit row" guarantee under a genuine two-process race is established by the disposable manual verification described above, not by a permanently-committed automated test — the same limitation this codebase's other concurrency closures (M8, DEADLOCK-1) already carry and document. DB-2 and every other finding from the same fresh forensic audit (UX-1, PROD-1, SEC-2, QA-1, QA-2, ARCH-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, REL-3, REL-4, OPS-1, OPS-2) remain open and unaddressed.

**This finding is CLOSED.** Do not reopen unless a future change causes a real regression. Every other finding surfaced by the fresh forensic audit this was discovered in (DB-2, UX-1, PROD-1, SEC-2, QA-1, QA-2, ARCH-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, REL-3, REL-4, OPS-1, OPS-2) remains open and is explicitly **not** addressed by this closure.

### DE1 — LeadSource With Inbound Conversation History Is Permanently Undeletable — **WORKING AS INTENDED / APPROVED / CLOSED**

Originally surfaced during C1's own architecture inspection (see C1's closure above), tracked as an open, explicitly-deferred item through every subsequent closure in this module since, and finally resolved via its own full Inspect → Impact Analysis → Product Decision cycle (Architecture and Implementation phases deliberately skipped — see Product Decision below). **Not a code defect. No implementation was performed.**

**Finding**: `App\Livewire\Platform\Clients\LeadSources::delete()` (fixed by C1 to check dependents safely rather than crash) permanently refuses to delete a `LeadSource` that has ever received one inbound WhatsApp/Messenger/Instagram message, because `conversations.lead_source_id` is `restrictOnDelete()` and no delete, soft-delete, archive, or detach path exists anywhere in the codebase for `Conversation`/`Message`. Unlike the other four dependents C1's fix checks (`leads`/`lead_imports` cascade and never block; `lead_source_costs`/`website_forms` can be individually cleared via existing tenant UI), a Conversation dependency has no way to ever be resolved — so once triggered, the block is permanent, not merely "blocked until cleanup."

**Fresh re-verification at this closure's own HEAD** (`35f1880e5a349ceffb87261d13cd93cc80e142b8`), via Inspect + Impact Analysis, both read-only:
- The guard (`Conversation::where('lead_source_id', $source->id)->exists()`, checked first among the five dependents, inside `DB::transaction()` + `lockForUpdate()`) is present, unchanged, and still matches C1's original fix exactly.
- Attempting to delete such a source shows a clear, controlled `Flux::toast('danger', ...)` and aborts cleanly — no crash, no partial write, no data loss; the source row and all its history remain completely untouched and fully usable.
- `LeadSource.is_active`/`toggleActive()` was confirmed, by direct code inspection (not assumption), to be a **functionally real retirement mechanism**: the webhook resolver's per-type `LeadSource::query()->where('type', $type)->where('is_active', true)->firstOrFail()` lookup (and the model's own `active()` scope) means a deactivated source genuinely stops accepting new inbound webhook activity — this is not merely a cosmetic flag.
- Historical `Conversation`/`Message` data remains fully preserved and accessible regardless of a source's active/inactive state — confirmed by the absence of any `is_active` (or equivalent) filter anywhere in the code paths that display or query conversation/message history; the one `is_active` check found elsewhere in `Show.php` filters `TenantUser` (assignable staff), unrelated to LeadSource/Conversation.
- `PlatformLeadSourceDeletionTest.php`: **11/11 passed**, unmodified, re-run fresh at this closure — including the two tests directly proving the Conversation-history block and that messages/conversation/lead all survive a blocked deletion attempt untouched.
- No new test was added: existing coverage for the deletion-block/data-preservation half, plus direct, unambiguous code inspection for the `is_active`-gates-webhook-resolution half (a single, non-branching `where()` clause with no subtle logic to empirically prove), were judged sufficient — adding a characterization test purely to document already-unambiguous, unchanged behavior was not considered a genuinely useful addition.

**Approved product decision** (explicit, from the user, not inferred): the current behavior is **intentional, not a defect**.
- A `LeadSource` with inbound Conversation/Message history **must** remain permanently undeletable — this is confirmed, approved product behavior, not a residual limitation awaiting a fix.
- `is_active`/`toggleActive()` (pre-existing, unmodified) is the **approved, sufficient retirement mechanism** for such a source — deactivation, not deletion, is how an admin retires it going forward.
- This rule applies to **existing and future** sources alike — no retrospective data migration, reconstruction, or backfill was performed or required.
- Historical Conversation/Message data **must** remain accessible for retired/deactivated sources — confirmed already true today, unconditionally, with no code change needed to guarantee it.
- **No archive entity, detach mechanism, purge workflow, or Conversation/Message schema change was introduced or is required.** `Conversation`, `Message`, their migrations, `LeadSource`, `LeadSourcePolicy`, and the C1 deletion guard itself are all **completely unmodified** by this closure — this is a documentation-only closure recording a product decision, not a code change.

**Explicitly confirmed at closure**: C1's deletion-guard fix is unchanged and not reopened. DB-1 (Lead-level hard-delete audit/consent preservation) is a distinct finding concerning a different entity (`Lead`, not `LeadSource`) and imposes no technical constraint on this decision — neither closure touches the other's files. No archive/detach/purge product direction (the alternatives analyzed and explicitly declined during Impact Analysis) was implemented. `git diff --check`: clean; this closure's diff is `docs/lead-management/PROGRESS.md` only.

**This finding is CLOSED — WORKING AS INTENDED.** Do not reopen unless a future change causes a real regression against the approved behavior above (i.e., unless the deletion guard, `is_active` gating, or history accessibility is later found broken — not merely because deletion is still unavailable, which is the explicitly approved, intended behavior). Every other finding from the fresh forensic audit (DB-2, UX-1, PROD-1, SEC-2, QA-1, QA-2, ARCH-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, REL-3, REL-4, OPS-1, OPS-2) remains open and is explicitly **not** addressed by this closure.

### SEC-2 — Legacy Website-Form Rate Limiting — **COMPLETE / APPROVED / CLOSED**

One of the findings surfaced by the same fresh forensic audit that produced SEC-1/REL-1/REL-2/DEADLOCK-1/EMAIL-LOSS-1/DB-1 above, investigated and closed via the full Inspect → Impact Analysis → Architecture/Decision Brief → Approval → Implementation → Testing → Regression → Final Verification → Approval sequence.

**Finding, confirmed**: `POST /api/webhook/website-form/{tenant_code}` (`App\Http\Controllers\Api\LeadWebhookController::websiteForm()`) had no request-rate ceiling of any kind — not a custom limiter, not even Laravel's generic default `throttle:api` (confirmed via direct read of the framework's own `Illuminate\Foundation\Configuration\Middleware` source: the `api` middleware group only gains a throttle entry if `->throttleApi()` is explicitly called, which this app's `bootstrap/app.php` never did). This was deliberate, not accidental — the route's own pre-existing comment and `AppServiceProvider::configureRateLimiting()`'s own docblock both already stated the legacy endpoint was left unthrottled when the sibling builder-form route (Feature #13) received rate limiting. REL-2's content-fingerprint idempotency was confirmed insufficient as a substitute — it only suppresses exact-content repeats within a 20-second window and does nothing against varied content.

**Remediation**: two new named `RateLimiter::for()` limiters — `legacy-website-form-per-source` (60 requests/minute) and `legacy-website-form-per-source-ip` (20 requests/minute) — both keyed on the resolved `LeadSource.id` (never the API key value, never a per-form token, since none exists on this route), attached to the legacy route only. A new, narrowly-scoped `App\Http\Middleware\ThrottleRequestsFailOpen` evaluates them instead of Laravel's stock `throttle:` middleware, because that stock middleware has no fail-open capability whatsoever (confirmed by direct read of its source — `tooManyAttempts()`/`hit()` are called with zero try/catch anywhere in the class, so a cache-backend failure there produces an uncaught 500, not a graceful fail-open). The new middleware wraps *only* those two cache-backend calls in a try/catch — resolving the named limiter, invoking its closure, and `$next($request)` all sit outside it — so a limiter-backend failure fails open (request proceeds, Lead still created) with a warning-level log, while a missing/unregistered named limiter (a configuration mistake, not a transient outage) is logged at error level instead, and any unrelated application/business/database exception is never misclassified as a limiter failure, since nothing but those two specific calls is inside the try block. An over-limit request receives `HTTP 429` before the controller is ever invoked, since rate limiting runs as route middleware — before `websiteForm()`'s own API-key check, domain check, and REL-2 idempotency.

**Verification**: `tests/Feature/Leads/LegacyWebsiteFormRateLimitTest.php` (new, 13 tests): **13/13 passed** — under-limit success; the exact 20/60 boundaries (21st and 61st requests rejected with no Lead created); independent per-IP buckets sharing one source-wide bucket; source isolation; tenant isolation; distinct-payload submissions still capped (not evaded by varying content); rapid identical submissions still counted by the rate limiter even though REL-2 independently still suppresses the duplicate Lead; `429` responses confirmed to never dispatch `LeadReceived`; rapid sequential dispatch counted with no off-by-one (single-process best-effort, not a cross-process race proof — documented as such in the test itself); a genuine limiter-backend failure (mocked) fails open with a warning logged and the Lead still created; an unregistered limiter name fails open with an error logged; and a dedicated test proving the fail-open try/catch's narrow scope holds — an exception thrown outside it (simulating an unrelated failure) propagates normally rather than being swallowed. Direct-neighbor suite (`WebsiteFormSubmissionTest`, `WebsiteFormCatalogTest`, `WebhookTest`, `WebsiteFormConsentSubmissionTest`, `WebsiteFormAdditionalDetailsTest`, plus the new file): **125/125 passed**, including every existing builder-route throttle test, unmodified and still passing — confirming no regression to Feature #13's own rate limiting. Broader `tests/Feature/Leads/` regression: **1085/1086 passed**, 1 known pre-existing environment-conditional skip (unchanged from the pre-SEC-2 baseline). `tests/Feature/Broadcasting/`: the same **2 known pre-existing, unrelated `LeadsIndexListenerTest` failures**, unchanged, not touched. Pint: clean. `git diff --check`: clean. PHPStan: 0 errors on all 4 touched production files; repo-wide total confirmed via `git stash` to be **identical with and without** the SEC-2 changes — SEC-2 itself adds **0 net-new diagnostics** (the baseline's own growth since DB-1/DE1 is pre-existing and unrelated, not attributable to this closure).

**Explicitly confirmed at closure**: `App\Http\Controllers\Api\LeadWebhookController.php` (REL-2's `withWebsiteFormIdempotency()` and everything else in that file) is completely untouched — REL-2 remains byte-for-byte unchanged. The builder route's own `website-form-per-form`/`website-form-per-tenant` limiters and its route/middleware definition are unchanged. Every other webhook handler (`facebookMessenger`, `instagram`, `whatsapp`, Facebook Lead Ads) is unchanged. No migration was added; `LeadSource` gained no columns. No trusted-source exemption, tier, allowlist, or admin UI was introduced — all legacy integrations receive uniform treatment, per the approved product decision. No CAPTCHA, honeypot, WAF, CDN throttling, Redis migration, or general anti-spam infrastructure was introduced.

**This finding is CLOSED.** Do not reopen unless a future change causes a real regression. Every other finding surfaced by the fresh forensic audit this was discovered in (DB-2, UX-1, PROD-1, QA-1, QA-2, ARCH-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, REL-3, REL-4, OPS-1, OPS-2) remains open and is explicitly **not** addressed by this closure.

### DB-2 — Concurrent CSV Duplicate-Email Race — **COMPLETE / APPROVED / CLOSED**

One of the findings surfaced by the same fresh forensic audit that produced SEC-1/REL-1/REL-2/DEADLOCK-1/EMAIL-LOSS-1/DB-1/DE1/SEC-2 above, investigated and closed via the full Inspect → Impact Analysis → Architecture/Decision Brief → Approval → Implementation → Testing → Regression → Final Verification → Approval sequence, including a follow-up reconciliation cycle for a query-budget regression discovered during this finding's own first Final Verification pass (see "Query-budget reconciliation" below), before final closure. The original forensic audit had flagged this race but stated it was not reproduced at the time; this closure reproduced it, confirmed it, and fixed it.

**Finding, confirmed**: `App\Actions\Tenant\CreateLeadFromImport::processRow()`'s own duplicate-email/phone check (`checkExistingLeadDuplicate()`) ran **before** the method's existing `Tenant::lockForUpdate()` coordination lock (the same tenant lock DEADLOCK-1 established across every Lead-creation path) — a genuine check-then-act (TOCTOU) race. Two concurrent CSV import jobs for the same tenant, both containing a row with the same normalized email/phone, could each independently see "no existing match," both pass the pre-lock check, and both proceed to create a Lead — producing two Lead rows for what should have been one Lead plus one recorded Duplicate. Reproduced 6/6 via a real two-process PostgreSQL race during Inspect, before any fix.

**Remediation**: the exact same, unmodified `checkExistingLeadDuplicate()` helper is called a second time, immediately after `Tenant::whereKey($tenant->id)->lockForUpdate()->firstOrFail()` inside `processRow()`'s existing `DB::transaction()`, before any other row resolution (status/priority/assignee/tags/currency) begins. Since every concurrent Lead-creation path for a tenant already serializes on this same tenant lock (DEADLOCK-1), whichever row's transaction commits first is guaranteed visible to the second row's re-check the moment it acquires the lock — no new lock primitive, no database unique constraint, no schema/migration change, and no retry loop were introduced. The pre-lock fast-path check (`checkInFileDuplicate()` ?? `checkExistingLeadDuplicate()`) is unchanged and kept deliberately as a non-authoritative fast path — cheap early rejection for the common (non-racing) case, with the in-lock re-check as the sole source of correctness. Existing normalized email/phone matching semantics, per-tenant scope, merged-lead exclusion (`whereNull('merged_into_lead_id')`), `Duplicate`/`duplicate_of_lead_id` import-row semantics, and same-file (in-CSV) duplicate behavior are all unchanged — the fix reuses the existing helper and existing recording path byte-for-byte. Every other Lead-creation path (Manual, Webhook, Email) is unchanged; INC-1 remains a separate, unresolved finding, untouched by this closure.

**Real PostgreSQL concurrency verification**: a disposable, non-committed `pcntl_fork()` script (not part of the repository, mirroring this codebase's established real-concurrency-verification precedent) ran genuinely independent OS processes against the real dev PostgreSQL connection, with a file-based ready/go barrier for simultaneous release rather than a sequential loop. **12/12 rounds passed**: 5 rounds of a 4-way same-email race (exactly 1 Lead created, 3 `Duplicate` rows, all `duplicate_of_lead_id` pointing to the single winner, 0 exceptions, every round); 2 rounds of case/format-varied same-email races (identical result); 3 rounds of 5-way concurrent *distinct*-email imports (5 Leads, 0 Duplicates, 0 exceptions/deadlocks — proving the tenant lock introduces no false positives); 2 stress rounds at N=8 same-email contenders (1 Lead, 7 Duplicates, 0 exceptions). The real dev database was independently confirmed, via read-only row-count checks before and after every run, to be left exactly as found.

**Query-budget reconciliation**: closing the race requires one additional `checkExistingLeadDuplicate()` call per row inside the lock, which costs one extra database query per email-only row (more if a row also has a phone). This shifted `LeadCsvImportEngineTest.php`'s pre-existing M6 query-count regression guard — a deliberately tight upper-bound ceiling, not an exact-count assertion, over a fixed 20-row deterministic fixture — from a measured 299 queries to a measured 319 (confirmed deterministic via `git stash` A/B, every run). The test's threshold was updated from `<310` to `<330`, preserving the exact same margins the original design used (+11 above the new baseline, 29 below the nearest possible M6-reversion regression total), so the guard's original discriminating power — catching either half of the M6 optimization being independently reverted — is fully preserved; only the DB-2-caused constant offset moved. This was an explicit, approved reconciliation decision, not an arbitrary loosening: the authoritative post-lock duplicate check was kept exactly as designed, and no query-count optimization was attempted at the expense of correctness.

**Verification**:
- `CreateLeadFromImportTest.php`: **28/28 passed** (26 pre-existing tests unmodified and still passing, plus 2 new: merged-lead exclusion under the CSV duplicate check, and cross-tenant duplicate-check isolation — both previously uncovered by name).
- `LeadCsvImportEngineTest.php`: **5/5 passed**, including the reconciled query-budget test (319 < 330).
- Real PostgreSQL concurrency: **12/12 rounds passed** (see above).
- `tests/Feature/Leads/`: **1088 passed**, 1 known pre-existing environment-conditional skip (unchanged).
- `tests/Feature/`: **1265 passed**, 2 known pre-existing, unrelated `Broadcasting\LeadsIndexListenerTest` failures (confirmed unchanged, not touched), 1 known skip.
- Pint: clean. `git diff --check`: clean throughout every pass.
- PHPStan: repo-wide baseline is **434 pre-existing errors on unmodified `main`**, confirmed identical with and without the full DB-2 diff via `git stash` — **0 net-new diagnostics**, and 0 errors on any of the three touched files individually.

**Explicitly confirmed at closure**: no new lock primitive was introduced — the fix reuses DEADLOCK-1's existing `Tenant::lockForUpdate()` exactly as-is. No database unique constraint or migration was introduced; a system-wide email-uniqueness redesign was evaluated during Impact Analysis and explicitly rejected as unnecessary — the confirmed defect was CSV import's own narrower creation-time check having a race, not an absence of system-wide prevention (which is this app's existing, deliberate design). `LeadDuplicateDetectionService`, `LeadMergeService`, `CreateManualLead`, `LeadWebhookController`, and `ProcessEmailLeadSource` are all confirmed completely untouched. INC-1 remains separate and unresolved, not addressed by this closure.

**Exact files changed**: `app/Actions/Tenant/CreateLeadFromImport.php` (the fix — one additional, unmodified-helper re-check call inside the existing lock), `tests/Feature/Leads/CreateLeadFromImportTest.php` (2 new tests), `tests/Feature/Leads/LeadCsvImportEngineTest.php` (query-budget threshold/derivation-comment reconciliation only).

**This finding is CLOSED.** Do not reopen unless a future change causes a real regression. Every other finding surfaced by the fresh forensic audit this was discovered in (UX-1, PROD-1, QA-1, QA-2, ARCH-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, REL-3, REL-4, OPS-1, OPS-2) remains open and is explicitly **not** addressed by this closure.

### REL-3 — First-Response Summary Service Range Guard — **COMPLETE / APPROVED / CLOSED**

One of the findings surfaced by the same fresh forensic audit that produced SEC-1/REL-1/REL-2/DEADLOCK-1/EMAIL-LOSS-1/DB-1/DE1/SEC-2/DB-2 above, investigated and closed via the full Inspect → Impact Analysis → Architecture/Decision Brief → Approval → Implementation → Testing → Regression → Final Verification → Approval sequence, including a mid-implementation formula correction discovered and resolved before Final Verification (see "Exclusive-`$to` semantics" below).

**Finding, confirmed as a defense-in-depth/service-boundary gap, not a currently-exploitable defect**: `App\Livewire\Tenant\LeadReports\Index`, the only production caller, already enforced a 92-day maximum date span server-side, on every request, via its own `rangeError()` check, before ever calling `App\Services\LeadReportingService::firstResponseSummary()` — the shipped feature was bounded. But `firstResponseSummary()` itself had no range enforcement of its own: a direct call with a 10-year range was empirically confirmed, during Inspect, to execute successfully as a single, real, unbounded SQL query. The gap was that this policy lived only in the presentation-layer caller, with nothing preventing a future caller (a new API endpoint, console command, or other reporting feature) from invoking the service directly and receiving whatever row volume an unbounded range produced, with no LIMIT, no chunking, and no independent safeguard.

**Remediation**: a single canonical constant, `LeadReportingService::MAX_RANGE_DAYS = 92`, is now the sole source of truth for the policy — `LeadReports\Index` references it directly instead of keeping its own independent copy. `firstResponseSummary()` gained a guard at the very start of the method, before `baseQuery()` or any query construction, rejecting `$from > $to` or an out-of-policy span by throwing a new, minimal `App\Exceptions\LeadReportingRangeException` (mirroring the existing `LeadMergeException` pattern exactly — a narrow `RuntimeException` subclass, no generic exception hierarchy introduced). The existing query (`baseQuery()`, selected columns, `whereNotNull('first_contacted_at')`), the PHP-side average/median computation, `rangeError()`'s own parsing/messages/short-circuit behavior, tenant isolation, and authorization are all completely unchanged — the guard is a pure addition, never reachable by the current caller (which already never sends an out-of-policy range), existing solely to protect a future one.

**Exclusive-`$to` semantics — a real mid-implementation correction, not a design afterthought**: the initially-implemented guard copied `rangeError()`'s own `diffInDays($to) + 1 > 92` formula verbatim, as originally instructed — but `rangeError()` computes that formula against the **raw, inclusive** `fromDate`/`toDate` a user actually selects, while `firstResponseSummary()`'s only real caller never passes those raw values: it passes `Index::from()`/`Index::to()`, and `Index::to()`'s own docblock is explicit that it returns an **exclusive** upper bound (`created_at < startOfDay(toDate + 1 day)`). Applying the identical `+1` formula to an already-exclusive `$to` double-counted the adjustment and was empirically proven, before this was caught, to wrongly reject the exact, currently-valid, currently-accepted 92-day UI selection. The corrected guard uses `$from->diffInDays($to) > self::MAX_RANGE_DAYS`, with **no `+1`** — verified empirically to agree exactly with `rangeError()`'s verdict at both boundaries (91-days-back-to-today: 92-day span, accepted by both; 92-days-back-to-today: 93-day span, rejected by both) — and the service method's own code comment now documents this exclusive-`$to` contract explicitly, so a future developer does not reintroduce the `+1` by analogy to `rangeError()`.

**Verification**:
- New focused tests in `LeadResponseTimeReportingTest.php` (6): a direct service call at the exact 92-day-equivalent boundary succeeds with unchanged output; a direct call at the 93-day-equivalent boundary throws `LeadReportingRangeException`; a direct call with `from > to` throws; an out-of-policy direct call is proven, via query-log inspection, to execute **zero** `leads`-table queries before throwing; and a combined test proves the Livewire-layer and service-layer verdicts agree exactly at both the 92- and 93-day boundaries.
- `LeadReportingTest.php` + `LeadResponseTimeReportingTest.php` + `LeadSourceCostReportingTest.php`: **46/46 passed**.
- `tests/Feature/Leads/`: **1093 passed**, 1 known pre-existing environment-conditional skip (unchanged).
- `tests/Feature/`: the same **2 known pre-existing, unrelated `Broadcasting\LeadsIndexListenerTest` failures** (confirmed identical and reproducible on unmodified `main` too); one run additionally surfaced the already-documented (Feature #11 closure, above), order-dependent `KanbanBoardTest` Faker-data-collision flake — reproduced 3/3 passing in isolation and confirmed non-deterministic across repeated runs both with and without this closure's diff present, not attributable to this closure.
- Pint: clean (one `phpdoc_align` auto-fix, alignment only, verified zero logic change). `git diff --check`: clean.
- PHPStan: repo-wide baseline is **434 pre-existing errors on unmodified `main`**, confirmed identical with and without the full REL-3 diff via `git stash -u` — **0 net-new diagnostics**.

**Explicitly confirmed at closure**: the 92-day policy itself was not changed, loosened, or reinterpreted — this closure only adds a second, independent enforcement point for the exact same, already-approved number. No row-count cap, no SQL aggregation redesign, no caching, and no change to any other `LeadReportingService` method (`volumeCount`, `dailyVolumeBreakdown`, `statusBreakdown`, `sourceBreakdown`, `assignmentBreakdown`, `sourceCostSummary`) were introduced — all confirmed untouched by diff inspection; those methods share the identical structural gap by inspection but remain explicitly out of this closure's scope. No migration, no schema change, no route or authorization change. REL-4 (a separate, unrelated `each()`/chunk-under-concurrent-delete finding) was not touched. This closure does not claim or enable support for reporting ranges longer than 92 days — that remains explicitly out of scope, undecided, and a matter for a future, separate product decision if it is ever raised.

**Exact files changed**: `app/Exceptions/LeadReportingRangeException.php` (new), `app/Services/LeadReportingService.php` (`MAX_RANGE_DAYS` constant + guard in `firstResponseSummary()` only), `app/Livewire/Tenant/LeadReports/Index.php` (constant reference only — `rangeError()`'s logic and messages unchanged), `tests/Feature/Leads/LeadResponseTimeReportingTest.php` (6 new tests).

**This finding is CLOSED.** Do not reopen unless a future change causes a real regression. Every other finding surfaced by the fresh forensic audit this was discovered in (UX-1, PROD-1, QA-1, QA-2, ARCH-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, REL-4, OPS-1, OPS-2) remains open and is explicitly **not** addressed by this closure.

### REL-4 — each()/OFFSET Pagination Skip Under Concurrent Delete — **COMPLETE / APPROVED / CLOSED**

One of the findings surfaced by the same fresh forensic audit that produced SEC-1/REL-1/REL-2/DEADLOCK-1/EMAIL-LOSS-1/DB-1/DE1/SEC-2/DB-2/REL-3 above, investigated and closed via the full Inspect → Impact Analysis → Architecture/Decision Brief → Approval → Implementation → Testing → Regression → Final Verification → Approval sequence.

**Finding, confirmed**: Laravel's `Illuminate\Database\Concerns\BuildsQueries::each()` (installed `laravel/framework v13.29.0`) is a thin wrapper around `chunk()`, which computes every page as a pure `OFFSET`/`LIMIT` derived only from the page number — `$offset = (($page - 1) * $count) + skip` — with no comparison to any previously-seen row. If a row from an already-processed page is deleted, or updated in a way that disqualifies it from the query's own `WHERE` clause (a Lead merge setting `merged_into_lead_id`, a status change moving a row out of a `whereNotIn`/`where('status', ...)` filter), before the next page is fetched, every remaining row shifts left by one position and the next fixed `OFFSET` silently skips exactly one row that was never processed — confirmed both by direct reading of the installed framework source and by a real reproduction against this repository's own `Lead` model during Inspect (deleting a row from a processed page caused a different, still-pending row to be silently skipped entirely).

**Affected production sites, confirmed realistic**: three Builder-level `each()` call sites, each iterating potentially-large, mutable, per-tenant result sets in a daily scheduled sweep, with a genuinely reachable concurrent-mutation trigger (routine, everyday Lead/Task deletion or Lead merging):
- `App\Console\Commands\SendLeadAlertNotifications::sweep()` — Lead-level meeting/follow-up alert iteration.
- `App\Console\Commands\NurtureInactiveLeads::sweepTenant()` — Lead-level inactivity-nurturing iteration.
- `App\Console\Commands\SendTaskAlertNotifications::sweep()` — Task-level due-date alert iteration.

**Explicitly evaluated and excluded from this closure's scope (Option A, approved)**: the `Tenant::query()->...->each()` iteration present in all three of the same command files (no `Tenant::delete()` feature exists anywhere in this codebase — structurally the same hazard, but with no currently-reachable trigger) and `App\Listeners\SendNewLeadNotification` (a real mechanism, but the iterated set — active Client Admins in one tenant — realistically never approaches the 1000-row `each()` page size, making the second-page trigger implausible today). Both were deliberately left unconverted, not overlooked — a defensible, evidence-backed scope decision, not an oversight.

**Remediation**: the three confirmed sites' `each()` calls were replaced with `chunkById(200, ...)`, the exact same ID-cursor pattern already established and proven safe in this codebase (`RecalculateLeadScores.php`, `DetectDuplicateLeads.php`, `Leads\Index::export()` — the last of which already proved `with()` eager-loading compatibility). `chunkById()` resumes each page from the last-seen row's own primary key (`WHERE id > $lastId`) rather than a page-number-derived offset, so a row disappearing from the query's matching set — for any reason, at any point — has zero effect on which rows the next page fetches. Every existing predicate (tenant scoping, `whereNotNull($dateColumn)`, `whereNull('merged_into_lead_id')`, `whereNotIn('status_id', $closedStatusIds)`, `where('status', Pending)`, `whereNotNull('due_date')`), every `with()` eager-load, and every callback's internal notification/dedup/task-creation logic were carried over unchanged — the only structural change is wrapping the existing per-row callback body in a `foreach` over each page's `Collection`. `NurtureInactiveLeads`'s existing per-lead `try`/`catch`-and-log was preserved exactly; `SendLeadAlertNotifications`/`SendTaskAlertNotifications`'s pre-existing absence of per-row exception handling was likewise preserved, not silently "improved" as an incidental side effect. Chunk size `200` matches the codebase's own existing `chunkById()` precedent rather than `each()`'s prior default of `1000`, per approved architecture. No observability (processed-row counts, logging, metrics) was added — explicitly kept out of scope by approved decision, since it is not necessary to close the confirmed defect and the fix itself makes the previously-silent skip scenario simply not occur.

**A small, fully-explained PHPStan side effect**: removing the closure's own `Lead $lead`/`Task $task` per-row type hint (replaced by `chunkById()`'s page-level `Collection` callback) caused Larastan to lose type inference for `$lead` in `SendLeadAlertNotifications.php` specifically — `Tenant::leads()`'s relation method has the same under-specified-generics gap already documented elsewhere in this module (`Tenant::tenantUsers()`), so the model type didn't flow through automatically the way it did for the other two files' `Lead::query()`/`Task::query()` static calls. Fixed with a `Collection` type-hint plus an inline `/** @var Lead $lead */` annotation restoring the exact type information `each()`'s own closure parameter previously provided — a type-annotation fix only, no callback-body or logic change. Net effect confirmed via `git stash`: 434 baseline diagnostics → 433 after this closure's full diff — zero net-new diagnostics, with one pre-existing, unrelated diagnostic (a mismatch specific to `each()`'s own generic signature) incidentally resolved as a natural consequence of `each()` no longer being called at all.

**Verification**:
- 9 new focused tests (3 per affected file) in `tests/Feature/Notifications/SendLeadAlertNotificationsTest.php`, `tests/Feature/Notifications/SendTaskAlertNotificationsTest.php`, `tests/Feature/Leads/LeadNurturingTest.php`: multi-page processing reaches every qualifying row (201 rows, one more than the 200-row page size, forcing a genuine second page); a row deleted mid-sweep from an already-processed page does not cause a later, still-pending row to be skipped; a row disqualified mid-sweep (Lead merge, Lead status closure, Task completion) does not cause a later row to be skipped; ascending primary-key processing order preserved; tenant isolation preserved; `with()` eager-loading remains N+1-free (query-count-guarded). All 43 pre-existing tests across the same three files re-run unmodified and still pass.
- Focused suite: **52/52 passed**.
- `tests/Feature/Notifications/`: **45/45 passed**.
- `tests/Feature/Leads/`: **1096 passed**, 1 known pre-existing environment-conditional skip (unchanged).
- `tests/Feature/`: **1279 passed**, 2 known pre-existing, unrelated `Broadcasting\LeadsIndexListenerTest` failures (confirmed unchanged), 1 known skip.
- Pint: clean. `git diff --check`: clean.
- PHPStan: 434 pre-existing errors on unmodified `main` → 433 with this closure's full diff (confirmed via `git stash`) — **0 net-new diagnostics** (see above for the one incidentally-resolved pre-existing diagnostic).

**Explicitly confirmed at closure**: tenant scoping, every existing filter, ascending processing order, chunk/batch behavior, eager loading, callback side effects (notification dispatch, dedup checks, Task creation), per-row exception-handling semantics (preserved per-file, not uniformly changed), transaction boundaries (none introduced), command completion behavior (`self::SUCCESS` unconditionally, unchanged), and queue behavior (not applicable — none of the three converted sites are queued) were all preserved exactly. No schema, migration, route, or authorization change. `SendNewLeadNotification.php`, `ReconcileOutboundMessageStatus.php`, `LeadAdminNotifier.php`, `FetchEmailLeads.php`, `RecalculateLeadScores.php`, `DetectDuplicateLeads.php`, and `Leads\Index.php` all confirmed completely untouched. No known REL-4 regression remains.

**Exact files changed**: `app/Console/Commands/SendLeadAlertNotifications.php`, `app/Console/Commands/NurtureInactiveLeads.php`, `app/Console/Commands/SendTaskAlertNotifications.php` (the fix — `each()` → `chunkById(200, ...)`, callback logic otherwise unchanged), `tests/Feature/Notifications/SendLeadAlertNotificationsTest.php`, `tests/Feature/Notifications/SendTaskAlertNotificationsTest.php`, `tests/Feature/Leads/LeadNurturingTest.php` (9 new tests).

**This finding is CLOSED.** Do not reopen unless a future change causes a real regression. Every other finding surfaced by the fresh forensic audit this was discovered in (UX-1, PROD-1, QA-1, QA-2, ARCH-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, OPS-1, OPS-2) remains open and is explicitly **not** addressed by this closure.

### QA-1 — Missing Assignment-Denial Authorization Test Coverage — **COMPLETE / APPROVED / CLOSED**

One of the findings surfaced by the same fresh forensic audit that produced SEC-1/REL-1/REL-2/DEADLOCK-1/EMAIL-LOSS-1/DB-1/DE1/SEC-2/DB-2/REL-3/REL-4 above, investigated and closed via the full Inspect → Impact Analysis → Architecture/Decision Brief → Approval → Implementation → Testing → Regression → Final Verification → Approval sequence.

**Finding, confirmed as a test-only coverage gap, not a production authorization defect**: `App\Policies\LeadPolicy::assign()` (`belongsToUserTenant()` && `isClientAdmin()` && `hasModulePermission('lead-management', 'update')`) is correctly, independently re-checked at write time — not merely relied upon for UI-hiding — in all three of this codebase's actual assignment-mutating entry points: `App\Livewire\Tenant\Leads\Show::assign()` and `App\Livewire\Tenant\Leads\Index::applyBulkAssign()`/`bulkUnassign()`. Exhaustive search confirmed, however, that **no test anywhere exercised any of these three write-time denial branches with an actually-denied actor** — every existing `assign()`/`applyBulkAssign()`/`bulkUnassign()` test used an authorized Client Admin. One existing test, `'bulk assign is Client-Admin-only'`, was found to be misleadingly named: it exercised only `openBulkAssignModal()`'s separate, earlier, non-enforcing UI guard, never `applyBulkAssign()`'s own `Gate::denies()` check — creating a false sense of security about what was actually verified.

**Remediation — test-only, zero production-code change**: added direct write-time denial tests for all three entry points, each calling the real action method directly (never stopping at `canAssign()`/`openBulkAssignModal()`'s earlier UI-hiding checks) with a genuine same-tenant, non-admin `clientUserWithLeadPermission()` actor, asserting the denial toast/skip, `assigned_to` left unchanged, and no `LeadAssignment`/notification/`LeadDetailUpdateLog` side effect. The bulk tests select an all-denied selection deliberately (`assign()`'s `isClientAdmin()`/`hasModulePermission()` conditions are actor-level, not per-Lead, so a mixed allowed/denied selection is not a scenario this specific policy can actually produce — a mixed-selection test would have drifted into testing an unrelated stale-selection mechanism, not this finding's boundary). The misleading test was renamed to `'a non-admin cannot open the bulk assign modal'` (assertions/behavior unchanged) with a comment pointing to the new, separately-named write-time test — the two concepts were deliberately kept in separate tests, never merged.

**Cross-tenant coverage — an architectural blocker discovered and handled by explicit approval, not worked around**: a `Show::assign()`-based cross-tenant Livewire test was attempted first, per the original approved design, and failed — empirically confirmed, not assumed — because `Show::mount()`'s own `Gate::authorize('view', $lead)` (the throwing form) shares the identical `belongsToUserTenant()` condition `assign()` itself checks, so a genuinely cross-tenant actor is always rejected by `view` before `assign()` is ever reached through the normal component lifecycle. Rather than bypass `mount()`, use reflection, or mutate private component state to force an unrealistic scenario, this was reported and an alternative was explicitly approved: a direct, policy/Gate-level regression test — `Gate::forUser($tenantBAdmin)->denies('assign', $leadA)` — exercising the real, unmodified `LeadPolicy::assign()` method through Laravel's own Gate facade (the identical mechanism every production call site uses), independently proving the tenant condition itself rather than inferring it from a different policy method's behavior.

**Verification**:
- 5 new/corrected tests total: 3 direct write-time denial tests (`Show::assign()`, `applyBulkAssign()`, `bulkUnassign()`), 1 direct Gate-level cross-tenant `LeadPolicy::assign()` denial test, 1 test rename (no behavior change).
- Focused (`LeadAssignmentTest.php` + `BulkActionsTest.php` + `LeadPermissionUiTest.php`): **48/48 passed**.
- `tests/Feature/Leads/`: **1100 passed**, 1 known pre-existing environment-conditional skip (unchanged).
- `tests/Feature/`: **1283 passed**, 2 known pre-existing, unrelated `Broadcasting\LeadsIndexListenerTest` failures (confirmed unchanged), 1 known skip.
- Pint: clean. `git diff --check`: clean.
- PHPStan: 433 pre-existing errors on unmodified `main` → 433 with this closure's full diff (confirmed via `git stash`) — **0 net-new diagnostics**.

**Explicitly confirmed at closure**: `LeadPolicy.php`, `Leads/Show.php`, `Leads/Index.php`, `LeadAssignmentService.php` all confirmed completely untouched — zero production-code change of any kind. `autoAssign()`/system-triggered assignment, every other `LeadPolicy` ability (`view`, `update`, `delete`, `merge`, `create`), and no migration/schema/route change. Existing authorized/happy-path assignment behavior (history, notifications, broadcasting, idempotency, auto-assign fairness) confirmed unchanged — every pre-existing test in both files re-ran unmodified and still passes. The cross-tenant limitation is explicit and permanent, not a residual gap: `Show::assign()`'s own tenant condition can never be independently exercised through the normal Livewire lifecycle (`view`'s identical, stricter-reached condition always fails first), so its only direct test coverage is, and is intended to remain, the policy/Gate-level regression test described above.

**Exact files changed**: `tests/Feature/Leads/LeadAssignmentTest.php` (+2 tests), `tests/Feature/Leads/BulkActionsTest.php` (+2 tests, 1 rename). No production file.

**This finding is CLOSED.** Do not reopen unless a future change causes a real regression. Every other finding surfaced by the fresh forensic audit this was discovered in (UX-1, PROD-1, ARCH-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, OPS-1, OPS-2) remains open and is explicitly **not** addressed by this closure. (QA-2 — a related but distinct finding scoped specifically to `Index::applyBulkAssign()` — was subsequently found to already be substantively satisfied by this closure's own test changes; see QA-2's own closure entry immediately below for the full reconciliation.)

### QA-2 — Index::applyBulkAssign() Permission-Denial Test Coverage — **CLOSED / ALREADY FIXED, ABSORBED BY QA-1**

A distinct finding from the same fresh forensic audit that produced SEC-1/REL-1/REL-2/DEADLOCK-1/EMAIL-LOSS-1/DB-1/DE1/SEC-2/DB-2/REL-3/REL-4/QA-1 above, closed via a dedicated Reconciliation/Inspection pass rather than a fresh Implementation cycle, since that reconciliation established no new code or test change was needed.

**Original finding** (Category: Testing/QA, Severity: LOW, Status: CONFIRMED, Confidence: HIGH): `Index::applyBulkAssign()` has a real, write-time permission-denial branch (`Gate::denies('assign', $lead)`, checked per-Lead inside its selection loop), but at the time of the original audit, no test exercised that branch with an actual denied actor — the original audit explicitly distinguished this from QA-1 (`Show::assign()`).

**Reconciliation, not reimplementation**: QA-1's own remediation (closed immediately above) was, at the time it was approved and implemented, scoped to *all three* assignment-mutating entry points — `Show::assign()`, `Index::applyBulkAssign()`, and `Index::bulkUnassign()` — because Inspect/Impact Analysis for QA-1 independently discovered the identical missing-denial-coverage pattern across all three, not only the one QA-1's own original text names. As a direct, incidental consequence, QA-1's approved test changes already added the exact test QA-2 itself calls for. This was not assumed from the tests' existence alone — a dedicated reconciliation pass independently verified, against the actual committed test body, that every literal element of QA-2's requirement is met: a genuine denied actor (`clientUserWithLeadPermission($tenant, ['view', 'update'])`, non-`isClientAdmin()`); `applyBulkAssign()` called directly, never via `openBulkAssignModal()`; the write-time `Gate::denies('assign', $lead)` branch as the only mechanism that could produce the asserted outcome for that actor; `assigned_to` asserted unchanged for every Lead in the selection; zero `LeadAssignment` rows; zero `LeadAssignedNotification` sent.

**No new implementation was performed for QA-2** — adding a second, near-duplicate test would have provided no additional coverage. `LeadPolicy.php`, `Leads/Show.php`, `Leads/Index.php`, `LeadAssignmentService.php`, and the existing QA-1 test files themselves were all confirmed untouched by this closure; QA-1 remains closed and was not reopened.

**Exact test satisfying QA-2**: `tests/Feature/Leads/BulkActionsTest.php`, `'a non-admin is denied applyBulkAssign() at the write-time Gate check, not just the closed modal'` — introduced by QA-1's own closure commit (`63dd8bf`), not by any QA-2-specific commit; re-run in isolation during this reconciliation and confirmed passing (1/1, 4 assertions).

**This finding is CLOSED — ALREADY FIXED, ABSORBED BY QA-1.** Do not reopen unless a future change causes a real regression to the cited test. Every other finding surfaced by the fresh forensic audit this was discovered in (UX-1, PROD-1, ARCH-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, OPS-1, OPS-2) remains open and is explicitly **not** addressed by this closure.

### ARCH-1 — Show.php Responsibility Concentration / Repeated Activity-Invalidation Knowledge — **COMPLETE / APPROVED / CLOSED**

One of the findings surfaced by the same fresh forensic audit that produced SEC-1/REL-1/REL-2/DEADLOCK-1/EMAIL-LOSS-1/DB-1/DE1/SEC-2/DB-2/REL-3/REL-4/QA-1/QA-2 above, investigated and closed via the full Inspect → Impact Analysis → Architecture/Decision Brief → Pre-Implementation Targeted Inspection → Approval → Implementation → Testing → Regression → Final Verification → Approval sequence.

**Original finding** (Category: Architecture, Severity: MEDIUM, Status: CONFIRMED, Confidence: HIGH): `App\Livewire\Tenant\Leads\Show` is a large (~2,927 lines, 97 public methods) Livewire component spanning many feature areas (contact/company/deal editing, status/assignment, notes, tags, consent, deals, tasks, merge/duplicate, messaging, custom fields, scoring). Line count and method count alone were explicitly not treated as a defect by themselves — investigation targeted concrete, provable maintainability costs instead.

**Confirmed concrete cost — repeated activity-invalidation knowledge**: 16 separate mutating methods across every one of the component's feature areas each independently repeated `unset($this->activity, ...)` to invalidate the `#[Computed]` `activity()` aggregator, meaning any future feature producing an activity-feed entry needed that same knowledge re-derived at its own call site, with no single place enforcing it.

**Approved remediation — Candidate A, centralized `refreshActivity()` helper**: broader decomposition (extracting Messaging/Custom Fields into child components, or splitting `Show` into multiple components) was evaluated in the Architecture/Decision Brief and explicitly **not** approved for this closure — deferred as a larger, separately-scoped future decision, not implemented here. The approved, narrower fix is a single new private method, `refreshActivity(): void`, containing only `unset($this->activity);`, called from all 16 sites that previously repeated that `unset()` inline. Every feature-specific computed property invalidated alongside `activity` at any of the 16 sites (`timeline`, `possibleDuplicates`, `customFieldDisplayRows`, `availableTagsToAttach`, `currentConsents`, `consentHistory`, `deals`, `currentDealId`, `tasks`) was deliberately left local to its own call site — via a second, narrower `unset(...)` statement immediately following `$this->refreshActivity();`, never merged into the helper — since each is feature-specific state with no relationship to the shared activity feed.

**Verification**:
- Focused tests across every feature group whose mutation path was among the 16 sites (detail editing, custom fields, status, assignment, merged status/assignment form, meeting/follow-up alert actions, notes, tag attach/detach, consent, deal lifecycle/management, task lifecycle/management, merge): **196/196 passed, 542 assertions**.
- `tests/Feature/Leads/`: **1100 passed**, 1 known pre-existing environment-conditional skip (unchanged), 0 failures.
- Pint: clean, zero changes needed.
- PHPStan: 434 pre-existing errors on unmodified `main` → 434 with this closure's full diff (confirmed via `git stash`) — **0 net-new diagnostics**, none referencing `Show.php` in either run.

**Explicitly confirmed at closure**: `activity()`'s own query/aggregation/output was never touched — behavior, ordering, and data sources are unchanged. No Blade file, Service, Action, other Livewire component, or any other finding's scope was touched. Status/Assignment logic itself was not refactored — only its pre-existing `unset($this->activity)` calls were replaced with a helper call. Only `app/Livewire/Tenant/Leads/Show.php` was modified — one new private method plus a mechanical transformation of the existing 16 call sites, no other cleanup or formatting refactor.

**Exact files changed**: `app/Livewire/Tenant/Leads/Show.php` only (41 insertions, 16 deletions).

**This finding is CLOSED — the narrower, approved centralization only.** Broader `Show.php` decomposition (Messaging/Custom Fields extraction as separate components, or any other structural split) remains explicitly un-approved and out of scope, not silently treated as complete. Do not reopen unless a future change causes a real regression. Every other finding surfaced by the fresh forensic audit this was discovered in (UX-1, PROD-1, ARCH-2, INC-1, OPS-1, OPS-2) remains open and is explicitly **not** addressed by this closure.
